We conclude this section by recalling the so-called ‘Gromov trick’, which forms the basis of much of this paper by establishing that we may use pseudo-holomorphic techniques to analyze the graphs of Floer-type cylinders.

Theorem 3.5

(1.4.C’. in
[
13
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
be an adapted homotopy of Floer data, then there exists a unique almost complex structure
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}
on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times M
with the property that a graph

\displaystyle\tilde{u}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times M

\displaystyle(s,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(s,t,u(s,t))

is
(j_{0},\tilde{\mathbb{J}})
-holomorphic if and only if
u
satisfies Equation
2
, where
j_{0}
denotes the standard complex structure on the cylinder.

The almost complex structure
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}
given by Theorem
3.5
is the unique almost complex structure such that:

1.

\tilde{\mathbb{J}}
restricts to
\mathbb{J}^{s}_{t}
on the tangent space of
\{(s,t)\}\times M
for each
(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
,

2.

The distribution

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathcal{H}}_{(s,t,p)}

\displaystyle:=\langle\partial_{s},\partial_{t}\oplus X^{\mathcal{H}^{s}}_{t}(p)\rangle\subset T_{(s,t,p)}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times M),

is invariant under
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}
,

3.

The projection map

\displaystyle pr_{cyl}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times M

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}

is
(\tilde{\mathbb{J}},j_{0})
-holomorphic.

Note that it follows immediately from the above that the fibers
\{(s,t)\}\times M
of
pr_{cyl}
form a
\tilde{\mathbb{J}}
-holomorphic foliation of
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times M
.

The the primary importance of the Gromov trick for our purposes comes from the fact that in dimension
4
, one has the phenomenon of
positivity of intersections
for pseudo-holomorphic curves, which we now recall for the convenience of the reader. Let
(M,J)
be an almost complex
4
-manifold,
(S_{i},j_{i})
(not necessarily closed) Riemannian surfaces and
u_{i}:(S_{i},j_{i})\rightarrow(M,J)

(j_{i},J)
-holomorphic maps for
i=0,1
with distinct images. If we denote by

\displaystyle I(u_{0},u_{1})

\displaystyle:=\{(z_{0},z_{1})\in S_{0}\times S_{1}:u_{0}(z_{0})=u_{1}(z_{1})\}

the pre-images of the intersection points of the images of the two pseudo-holomorphic curves, then the Carleman similarity principle implies that points of
I(u_{0},u_{1})
are isolated in
S_{0}\times S_{1}
. This implies that for any
(z_{0},z_{1})\in I(u_{0},u_{1})
, we may define
\iota(u_{0},u_{1};z_{0},z_{1})\in\mathbb{Z}
— the
local intersection number
of
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
at
(z_{0},z_{1})
— by fixing a contractible open set
V\subset M
containing
u_{0}(z_{0})=u_{1}(z_{1})
(and no other intersection points of the two curves), along with small disks
D_{i}\subset u_{i}^{-1}(V)
centered at
z_{i}
, and
C^{\infty}
-small perturbation
v
of
u_{0}
such that
v(\partial D_{0})
and
u_{1}(\partial D_{1})
are disjoint,
v|_{D_{0}}
is transverse to
u_{1}|_{D_{1}}
, and
v
agrees with
u_{0}
outside
D_{0}
. We then set

\displaystyle\iota(u_{0},u_{1};z_{0},z_{1})

\displaystyle:=v_{0}\cdot_{V}u_{1},

where
v_{0}\cdot_{V}u_{1}
denotes the usual intersection number of the maps
v_{0}|_{D_{0}}
and
u_{1}|_{D_{1}}
, viewed as maps into
V
. The contractibility of
V
assures that this definition is independent of the choices made in this construction. For our purposes, positivity of intersections for pseudo-holomorphic curves may then be phrased as follows.

Theorem 3.6

(Positivity of intersections)
.

Let
(M,J)
be an almost complex
4
-manifold,
(S_{i},j_{i})
(not necessarily closed) Riemannian surfaces and
u_{i}:(S_{i},j_{i})\rightarrow(M,J)

(j_{i},J)
-holomorphic maps for
i=0,1
. Suppose that
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
have distinct images, then for every
(z_{0},z_{1})\in I(u_{0},u_{1})
, we have that
\iota(u_{0},u_{1};z_{0},z_{1})\geq 1
and moreover
\iota(u_{0},u_{1};z_{0},z_{1})=1
if and only if the intersection of
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
at
z_{0}
and
z_{1}
respectively is transverse.

We refer the interested reader to Appendix E of
[
31
]
for a proof and further details on positivity of intersections.

3.2
Asymptotic analysis for pseudoholomorphic cylinders

The main analytic fact that gives us control over the asymptotic winding behaviour of Floer cylinders, as well as that of vector fields lying in the kernel of the Floer differential, is the following theorem which describes the asymptotic behaviour of solutions to an appropriately perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation. This result is originally due to
[
33
]
, although the version we reproduce here for the convenience of the reader is from the appendix of
[
46
]
.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
w:[0,\infty)\times S^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfy the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{s}w+J_{0}\partial_{t}w+(S(t)-\Delta(s,t))w

\displaystyle=0,

(4)

where
S:S^{1}\rightarrow End(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is a smooth family of symmetric matrices and

\displaystyle\Delta:[0,\infty)\times S^{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow End(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

is smooth. Suppose that for
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2},
there exist constants
M_{\beta},d>0
such that

\displaystyle|(\partial^{\beta}\Delta)(s,t)|

\displaystyle\leq M_{\beta}e^{-ds},\;\text{and}

\displaystyle|(\partial^{\beta}w)(s,t)|

\displaystyle\leq M_{\beta}e^{-ds}.

Then either
w\equiv 0
or
w(s,t)=e^{\lambda s}(\xi(t)+r(s,t))
,
where
\lambda
is a negative eigenvalue of the self-adjoint operator

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}:H^{1}(S^{1};\mathbb{R}^{2n})\subseteq L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{R}^{2n})

\displaystyle\rightarrow L^{2}(S^{1};\mathbb{R}^{2n})

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\mapsto-J_{0}(\partial_{t}-J_{0}S)h,

\xi:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2n}
is an eigenvector of
\mathbf{A}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, and
r
satisfies the decay estimates

\displaystyle|(\partial^{\beta}r)(s,t)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-d^{\prime}s}M_{\beta}^{\prime}

for
d^{\prime},M_{\beta}^{\prime}>0
, for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
.

This theorem is useful in the following setting.
Let
(H,J)
be Floer regular. To any
x\in Per_{0}(H)
, we may assign the
asymptotic operator

\displaystyle A_{x,J}:\Gamma(x^{*}TM)\rightarrow\Gamma(x^{*}TM)

as follows. Let
\mathcal{V}:=\ker Dpr_{S^{1}}
denote the vertical tangent bundle of
S^{1}\times M
, where
pr_{S^{1}}:S^{1}\times M\rightarrow S^{1}
denotes the obvious projection map. Viewing
\xi\in\Gamma(x^{*}TM)
as a section of the vertical tangent bundle
\mathcal{V}|_{\check{x}}:=\check{x}^{*}\mathcal{V}
along the graph
\check{x}
of
x
, we let
\check{X}_{H}:=\partial_{t}\oplus X_{H}\in\mathcal{X}(S^{1}\times M)
, and we view
J=(J_{t})_{t\in S^{1}}
as an endomorphism of the vertical tangent bundle by setting
\check{J}_{t,x}:=J_{t}(x)
.
A_{x,J}
is then defined by setting
A_{x,J}(\xi):=-\check{J}\mathcal{L}_{\check{X}_{H}}\xi
, where
\mathcal{L}_{X}Y
denotes the Lie derivative of
Y
along
X
.
A_{x,J}
extends to an unbounded self-adjoint operator with discrete spectrum (still denoted
A_{x,J}
) from
W^{1,2}(x^{*}TM)
to
L^{2}(x^{*}TM)
.

By taking an exponential chart as in Section
2.2
on a neighbourhood
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}
of
\hat{x}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})
, Floer’s equation may be written in the local coordinates provided by this chart in the form of Equation
4
, with
A_{x,J}
being sent via these coordinates to
\mathbf{A}
. Following
[
46
]
, we make the following definition.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{L}_{0}(M)
and suppose that
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}u_{s}\equiv x
for a map
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow M
. For any
R>0
, a map

\displaystyle U^{+}:[R,\infty)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Gamma(x^{*}TM)

will be said to be a
positive asymptotic representative
of
u
if

u(s,t)=Exp(U^{+}(s))(t)
for all
(s,t)\in[R,\infty)\times S^{1}
, where
Exp
is as in Section
2.2
. The notion of a
negative asymptotic representative
of
u
,

\displaystyle U^{-}:(-\infty,-R]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Gamma(x^{*}TM)

is defined in the obvious analogous manner.

Every Floer-type cylinder considered in this paper admits, due to exponential convergence at the ends, essentially unique positive and negative asymptotic representatives, determined up to a restriction of the domains of
U^{\pm}
to larger values of
|R|
.

The main result that we will need from
[
46
]
is the following (this is Theorem
2.2
of the quoted work, paraphrased for our setting).

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
(H,J)
be a non-degenerate Floer pair,
x\in Per_{0}(H)
and let
u,v
solve Equation
2
for
s>>0
(resp. for
s<<0
), where the adapted homotopy used in defining Equation
2
satisfies
(H^{+},J^{+})=(H,J)
(resp.
(H^{-},J^{-})=(H,J)
). Suppose moreover that
u_{s}
and
v_{s}
both converge to
x
as
s\rightarrow\infty
(resp.
s\rightarrow-\infty
). Let
U
and
V
be positive (resp. negative) asymptotic representatives of
u
and
v
respectively. Then either
U\equiv V
or there exists a strictly negative (resp. strictly positive) eigenvalue
\lambda\in\sigma(A_{x,J})
and an eigenvector
\xi
with eigenvalue
\lambda
such that

\displaystyle(U-V)(s,t)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda s}(\xi(t)+r(s,t)),

where the remainder term satisfies the decay estimates
|\nabla_{s}^{i}\nabla_{t}^{i}r(s,t)|\leq M_{ij}e^{-ds}
for all
(i,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and some
M_{i,j},d>0
(resp.
d<0
).

Whenever
u,v
and
x
are as above with
u
and
v
restricting to distinct maps outside of every compact set of
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
, we will write
\xi^{+}_{u,v}
(resp.
\xi^{-}_{u,v}
) for the eigenvectors of
A_{x,J}
whose existence is guaranteed by the above theorem. We will call
\xi^{\pm}_{u,v}
the
positive (resp. negative) asymptotic eigenvector of
v
relative
u
. Note that the above result only requires that
u
and
v
solve Equation
2
on some neighbourhood of
s=\infty
(resp.
s=-\infty
), and that, for
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
, the trivial cylinder
v(s,t)=x(t)
is always a solution to Equation
2
outside some compact set. Provided
u
is not the trivial cylinder
u(s,t)=x(t)
, we will write
\xi^{\pm}_{u}:=\xi^{\pm}_{u,x}
and call these the (positive and negative)
asymptotic eigenvectors of
u
.

This asymptotic information becomes especially useful when combined with the following fact (see
[
16
]
p. 285 or
[
46
]
p.1637).

Proposition 3.10
.

If
\xi\in\Gamma(x^{*}TM)
is an eigenvector of
A_{x,J}
(and hence is not identically zero) then for all
t\in S^{1}
,
\xi(t)\neq 0
.

Since Theorem
3.9
tells us that for sufficiently large
\lvert R\rvert\in\mathbb{R}
the difference between the asymptotic representatives of Floer-type cylinders behaves to leading order like an eigenvector of
A_{x,J}
, the fact that these are nowhere vanishing implies:

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
u,v:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow M
be distinct finite energy solutions of Equation
2
, then there is a compact subset
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
such that
u(s,t)=v(s,t)
only if
(s,t)\in K
.

These results become even stronger in the case when
\dim M=2
, as in this case Proposition
3.10
implies that eigenvectors of the asymptotic operator have a well-defined winding number, once we fix a trivialization of
x^{*}TM
via a choice of capping disk. More precisely, when
M=\Sigma
, if
\hat{x}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{L}_{0}}(\Sigma)
, and
T_{\hat{x}}:S^{1}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2},\omega_{0})\rightarrow(x^{*}T\Sigma,\omega)
is a symplectic trivialization as in Section
2
, then for any eigenvector
\xi
of
A_{x,J}
, the map
t\mapsto T_{\hat{x}}(t)^{-1}\xi(t)
has a well-defined winding number
wind(\xi;\hat{x})
, by Proposition
3.10
. Proposition
2.22
then gives the following corollary.

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
u,v
be distinct finite energy solutions of Equation
2
with

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}u_{s}

\displaystyle=\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}v_{s}=x.

Then there exists
R>0
such that for all
s<-R
and any capping
\hat{x}=[x,\alpha]
, we have

\displaystyle\ell(\hat{v}_{s}^{\alpha},\hat{u}^{\alpha}_{s})

\displaystyle=wind(\xi_{u,v}^{-};\hat{x}),

where
\hat{u}^{\alpha}_{s}
(resp.
\hat{v}^{\alpha}_{s}
) denotes the capping of
u_{s}
(resp.
v_{s}
) such that
[x,\alpha]
and
\hat{u}_{s}
(resp.
\hat{v}_{s}
) are
0
-homotopic. The analogous statement when
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}u_{s}=\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}v_{s}=x
also holds.

If we combine the positivity of intersection of holomorphic curves in dimension
4
with the foregoing discussion, we arrive at the principal point of this section (recall from the discussion following Proposition
2.22
that
\ell(\hat{x},\hat{y}):=L_{0}(\hat{0},\{\hat{x},\hat{y}\})
).

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be non-degenerate Floer pairs,
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
and let
u,v\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)
be distinct finite energy solutions to Equation
2
for
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
. Then for any lifts
\hat{u},\hat{v}
of
u,v:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{0}(\Sigma)
, the function
\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}}(s):=\ell(\hat{u}_{s},\hat{v}_{s})
is non-decreasing, locally constant, and well-defined for all but finitely many values
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
s,s^{\prime}\in dom(\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}})
, with
s<s^{\prime}
,
\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}}(s)\neq\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}}(s^{\prime})
if and only if there exists
s_{0}\in(s,s^{\prime})
and some
t_{0}\in S^{1}
such that
u(s_{0},t_{0})=v(s_{0},t_{0})
.

Proof.

That
\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}}
has only finitely many points at which it is ill-defined follows the fact that, by definition,
\ell(\hat{u}_{s_{0}},\hat{v}_{s_{0}})
is undefined only when there exists
t_{0}\in S^{1}
such that
u(s_{0},t_{0})=v(s_{0},t_{0})
. By Corollary
3.11
, the set of all such
(s_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
must lie inside some compact set, and we may then apply Theorem
3.5
to choose an almost complex structure on
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma
such that the graphs
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{v}
are pseudoholomorphic, whence all such intersections must be isolated, and so finite in number. That
\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}}
is non-decreasing follows by applying the positivity of intersections for holomorphic curves in dimension
4
at these intersections, which implies moreover that any such intersection contributes strictly positively to the change in
\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}}(s)
as
s
increases and passes from one connected component of
dom(\ell_{\hat{u},\hat{v}})
to another.
∎

Definition 3.14
.

For
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
non-degenerate Floer pairs,
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
and
u,v\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x},\hat{y};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
,
u\neq v
, we define

\displaystyle\ell_{\pm\infty}(u,v)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{s\rightarrow\pm\infty}\ell(\hat{u}_{s},\hat{v}_{s}),

where
\hat{u}_{s}
and
\hat{v}_{s}
are the natural cappings of
u_{s}
and
v_{s}
(cf. Definition
2.16
).

Remark 7
.

We recall here for the convenience of the reader that the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\hat{x},\hat{y};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
contains, by definition, only cylinders which are
0
-homotopies between
\hat{x}
and
\hat{y}
(cf. Section
3.1
and
3.1.1
). This ensures in particular that the notion of “the natural cappings of
\hat{u}_{s}
and
\hat{v}_{s}
” used above is well-defined.

Note that the previous lemma implies that these quantities exist and are finite. Indeed, if
\hat{u}_{s}
and
\hat{v}_{s}
tend to
\hat{x}
as
s\rightarrow\pm\infty
, then
\ell_{\pm\infty}(u,v)=wind(\xi^{\pm}_{u,v};\hat{x})
by Corollary
3.12
, while if
\lim_{s\rightarrow\pm\infty}u_{s}=\hat{x}
and
\lim_{s\rightarrow\pm\infty}v_{s}=\hat{y}
with
x\neq y
, then
\ell_{\pm\infty}(u,v)=\ell(\hat{x},\hat{y})
.

3.2.1
Winding of eigenvectors of
A_{x,J}

We summarize here some necessary facts from
[
16
]
on the winding numbers of the eigenvectors of
A_{x,J}
which appeared in the previous subsection (while
[
16
]
works in the aspherical case, our previous discussion makes clear how this winding number depends on the choice of cappings of
x
and
y
and this is all that is needed to extend the results to capped orbits). For a loop
x\in\mathcal{L}_{0}(\Sigma)
, let
\pi_{2}(\Sigma;x)
denote the set of homotopy classes of capping disks for
x
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
(H,J)
be a non-degenerate Floer pair,
x\in Per_{0}(H)
with
H
non-degenerate and
J:S^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega)
arbitrary. There is a well-defined function

\displaystyle W=W_{x,J}:\pi_{2}(\Sigma;x)\times\sigma(A_{x,J})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle(\alpha,\lambda)

\displaystyle\mapsto wind(T_{[x,\alpha]}^{-1}\circ\xi),

where
\xi\in\Gamma(x^{*}T\Sigma)
is any eigenvector with eigenvalue
\lambda
. Moreover,
W
satisfies the following properties

1.

For any
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(\Sigma;x)
,
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}\;\Rightarrow\;W(\alpha,\lambda)\leq W(\alpha,\lambda^{\prime})
.

2.

For any
\alpha\in\pi_{2}(\Sigma;x)
, and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\sum_{\lambda\in W_{\alpha}^{-1}(k)}\dim E_{\lambda}=2
,

where
W_{\alpha}(\lambda)=W(\alpha,\lambda)
, and
E_{\lambda}
is the eigenspace associated to the eigenvalue
\lambda
.

3.

For any
A\in\pi_{2}(\Sigma)
,
W(A\cdot\alpha,\lambda)=W(\alpha,\lambda)+c_{1}(A)
.

In view of the control over the sign of the eigenvalue provided by Theorem
3.9
, combined with the monotonicity of the winding number provided by item
(1)
of the above proposition, we follow
[
16
]
in making the following definition.

Definition 3.16
.

For
(H,J)
and
\hat{x}=[x,\alpha]\in Per_{0}(H)
as above, define

\displaystyle a(\hat{x})=a(\hat{x};H)

\displaystyle:=\max_{\lambda\in\sigma(A_{x,J})\cap(-\infty,0)}W(\alpha,\lambda)

\displaystyle b(\hat{x})=b(\hat{x};H)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\lambda\in\sigma(A_{x,J})\cap(0,\infty)}W(\alpha,\lambda).

Remark that by the monotonicity of
W
and by Lemma
3.13
(see the remark following Definition
3.14
) we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.17
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be non-degenerate Floer pairs,
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
,
x^{\pm}_{i}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H^{\pm}})
for
i=0,1
.

1.

If
u_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}_{0}^{-},\hat{x}_{i}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
,
i=0,1
, then
b(\hat{x}_{0}^{-})\leq\ell_{-\infty}(u_{0},u_{1})
.

2.

If
u_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}_{i}^{-},\hat{x}_{0}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
,
i=0,1
, then
\ell_{\infty}(u_{0},u_{1})\leq a(\hat{x}^{+}_{0})
.

The result which relates this discussion to the behaviour of the Floer complex is the following:

Theorem 3.18

(
[
16
]
Theorem 3.10)
.

\displaystyle-\mu(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=2a(\hat{x})+p(\hat{x}),

(5)

where
p([x,\alpha])=0
if there exists
\lambda\in\sigma(A_{x,J})\cap(0,\infty)
such that
W(\alpha,\lambda)=a(\hat{x})
and
p([x,\alpha])=1
otherwise.

Remark 8
.

Note that our sign convention for the Conley-Zehnder index is the negative of the convention adopted in
[
16
]
.

Corollary 3.19
.

\displaystyle-\mu(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=a(\hat{x})+b(\hat{x}).

Proof.

The point is that
b(\hat{x})=a(\hat{x})+p(\hat{x})
. To see this, write
\hat{x}=[x,\alpha]
, and note first that
b(\hat{x})\in\{a(\hat{x}),a(\hat{x})+1\}
. This follows directly from the definitions of
a(\hat{x})
and
b(\hat{x})
combined with the surjectivity of
W(\alpha;-):\sigma(A_{x,J})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
which is implied by item 2 of Proposition
3.15
, together with the monotonicity of
W(\alpha;-)
and the fact that
0\not\in\sigma(A_{x,J})
when
H
is non-degenerate. By Theorem
5
,
p(\hat{x})=0
implies that
b(\hat{x})\leq a(\hat{x})
. On the other hand, if
p(\hat{x})=1
, then Theorem
5
implies that
b(\hat{x})>a(\hat{x})
. In either case, we obtain
b(\hat{x})=a(\hat{x})+p(\hat{x})
.
∎

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

1.

If
\mu(\hat{x};H)\in\{2k-1,2k\}
, then
a(\hat{x};H)=-k
.

2.

If
\mu(\hat{x};H)\in\{2k,2k+1\}
, then
b(\hat{x};H)=-k
.

Proof.

We prove (1) with the argument for (2) being entirely analogous (but additionally making use of the fact that
b(\hat{x})=a(\hat{x})+p(\hat{x})
established in the proof of Corollary
3.19
). Theorem
3.18
states that
-\mu(\hat{x})=2a(\hat{x})+p(\hat{x})
, where
p(\hat{x})\in\{0,1\}
is the parity of
\mu(\hat{x})
. Consequently, if
\mu(\hat{x})=2k-1
, we see that

\displaystyle 1-2k

\displaystyle=2a(\hat{x})+1

and therefore
a(\hat{x})=-k
. Similarly, if
\mu(\hat{x})=2k
, then

\displaystyle-2k

\displaystyle=2a(\hat{x})

and so
a(\hat{x})=-k
, as claimed.
∎

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be non-degenerate Floer pairs,
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
and suppose that
\hat{x}^{\pm}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H^{\pm}})
satisfy
\mu(\hat{x}^{\pm})=2k+1
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
|\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})|\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exist
u,v\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
,
u\neq v
. Then we have by Lemma
3.13
and Lemma
3.20

\displaystyle-k=b(\hat{x}^{-})\leq\ell_{-\infty}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{\infty}(u,v)\leq a(\hat{x}^{+})=-k-1,

which is a contradiction. The lemma follows.
∎

Recall from Section
3.1
that to any
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
, we associate the operator
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
. Whenever
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}(u)=0
, for
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)_{\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+}}
,
T_{u}\mathcal{E}
splits canonically as
T_{u}C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)_{\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+}}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{u}
. In such a case, we denote by
D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}
the projection of the differential of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}
onto
\mathcal{E}_{u}
, and we call
D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}
the
linearized Floer operator
. The transversality of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}
to the
0
-section of
\mathcal{E}
at
u
is equivalent to the surjectivity of
(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
, which is in turn related to the behaviour of its kernel by the Fredholm property.
The following result is essentially proved in
[
16
]
as Proposition
5.6
and serves to give significant control over elements in the kernel of the linearized Floer operator. We give a simple proof here in the Floer-theoretic setting for the convenience of the reader.

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be non-degenerate Floer pairs, let
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
,
u\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
, and let
\xi\in\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
. Suppose that
\xi\not\equiv 0
and denote by
Z(\xi)
the algebraic count of the number of zeros of
\xi
, then
Z(\xi)
is finite and satisfies the inequality
0\leq Z(\xi)\leq a(\hat{x}^{+})-b(\hat{x}^{-})
. Moreover,
Z(\xi)=0
if and only if
\xi
is nowhere vanishing.

Proof.

It is a standard result in Floer theory (see for instance
[
41
]
, Section
2.2
) that for any
u\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
, any element
\xi\in\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
may be expressed (with respect to the unitary trivialization
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times(\mathbb{R}^{2},J_{0})\rightarrow u^{*}(T\Sigma,J)
along
u
induced by the cappings of
\hat{x}^{-}
and
\hat{x}^{+}
) as solving an equation of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\xi+J_{0}\partial_{t}\xi+S\xi

\displaystyle=0,

where we may write
S
on the positive and negative ends as

\displaystyle S^{\pm}(s,t)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{-1}A_{x^{\pm},J}-\Delta^{\pm}(s,t),

with
\Delta^{\pm}
satisfying the decay estimates of Theorem
3.7
. Consequently, any
\xi\in\ker(D\mathcal{F})_{u}
that is not identically zero must be non-vanishing outside of some compact neighbourhood of
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
, and the Carlemann similarity principle implies that any zeros of such a
\xi
are isolated and must contribute strictly positively to
Z(\xi)
. Consequently, we see that
Z(\xi)
is finite and non-negative, with
Z(\xi)=0
exactly when
\xi
is nowhere vanishing.

To see that
Z(\xi)\leq a(\hat{x}^{+})-b(\hat{x}^{-})
, we take
R>0
sufficiently large so that
\xi
is non-vanishing outside of
(-R,R)\times S^{1}
and consider the homotopy of
2
-braids in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
induced by
h(s)=(0,\Phi^{-1}\xi_{s})\in\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})^{2}
,
s\in[-R,R]
. Theorem
3.7
implies that for
R>0
sufficiently large,

\displaystyle\ell(0,\xi_{-R})=wind(\Phi^{-1}\xi_{-R})

\displaystyle\geq b(\hat{x}^{-}),\;and

\displaystyle\ell(0,\xi_{R})=wind(\Phi^{-1}\xi_{R})

\displaystyle\leq a(\hat{x}^{+}),

and the algebraic count zeros of
\xi
correspond to the algebraic count of the intersections of the graphs of the strands of
h
from which the proposition follows.
∎

The following corollary is essentially the linear analogue of Corollary
3.21
.

Corollary 3.23
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be Floer regular, let
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
, and let
\hat{x}^{\pm}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H^{\pm}})
have
\mu(\hat{x}^{\pm})=2k+1
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
is
(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+})
-regular.

Proof.

If
(H^{-},J^{-})=(H^{+},J^{+})
and
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
is the constant homotopy of Floer data, then this is automatic by Floer regularity of
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
. The statement is also vacuously true if
\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
is empty. So we may suppose that
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
is not
\mathbb{R}
-invariant and that there exists some
u\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
. To see that
u
is regular, note that
D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}}
has Fredholm index
0
, and so to prove that
(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
is surjective, it suffices to show that its kernel is trivial. Suppose to the contrary that
\xi\in\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
is a non-trivial vector field along
u
. By the previous proposition, we must have that the algebraic count of its zeros
Z(\xi)
satisfies

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle Z(\xi)\leq a(\hat{x}^{+})-b(\hat{x}^{-})=-k-(k-1)=-1,

where the second to last equality follows from Lemma
3.20
. Clearly this is a contradiction, so we conclude that
\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}=0
and so
u
is regular.
∎

In the case of even index orbits, we have a somewhat weaker conclusion that will still be of use to us.

Corollary 3.24
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be Floer regular, let
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}
, and let
\hat{x}^{\pm}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H^{\pm}})
have
\mu(\hat{x}^{\pm})=2k
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then for every
u\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
,

\displaystyle\dim\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}

\displaystyle\leq 1

Proof.

As in the proof of the previous corollary, it suffices to consider the case where
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
is not
\mathbb{R}
-invariant and
\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
is non-empty. Letting
u\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
, consider the behaviour of some
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
. Let

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\rightarrow u^{*}T\Sigma

be a unitary trivialization of the tangent bundle along
u
which extends over the cappings of
\hat{x}^{\pm}
. Since
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
satisfy an appropriate perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation, Theorem
3.7
implies that
v_{i}(s,t):=(\Phi^{-1}\circ\eta_{i})(s,t)
,
i=1,2
satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}v_{i}(s,t)=\Phi^{-1}(-\infty,t)\circ\xi_{i}(t)

for
\xi_{i}\in\Gamma^{\infty}((x^{-})^{*}T\Sigma)
an eigenvector of the asymptotic operator
A_{x^{-},J^{-}}
associated to
x^{-}
, whose associated eigenvalue is positive. Let
\lambda_{0}
be the smallest positive eigenvalue of
A_{x^{-},J^{-}}
and note that every eigenvector
\xi^{\prime}\in E_{\lambda_{0}}
is such that
\Phi^{-1}(-\infty,t)\circ\xi^{\prime}(t)
has winding number
-k
by Corollary
3.19
, and by the same reasoning as in the proof of that corollary,
\dim E_{\lambda_{0}}=1
. We claim first that
\xi_{i}\in E_{\lambda_{0}}
for
i=1,2
. Indeed, suppose not, then without loss of generality we may suppose that
\xi_{1}\in E_{\lambda}
for
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
. This implies that
wind(\Phi^{-1}(-\infty,t)\circ\xi_{1}(t))\geq-k+1
, by points
(1)
and
(2)
of Proposition
3.15
combined with the fact that
a(\hat{x}^{-})=b(\hat{x}^{-})
(so the only other eigenspace whose eigenvector have winding
-k
is the eigenspace associated to the largest negative eigenvalue of
A_{x^{-},J^{-}}
). Whence,

\displaystyle wind(\Phi^{-1}(-R,t)\circ\eta_{1}(-R,t))

\displaystyle\geq-k+1

for
R>0
sufficiently large. As in the proof of Proposition
3.22
, we have that

\displaystyle Z(\eta_{1})

\displaystyle\leq a(\hat{x}^{+})-wind(\Phi^{-1}(-R,t)\circ\eta_{1}(-R,t))

for
R>0
sufficiently large, but this implies

\displaystyle Z(\eta_{1})

\displaystyle\leq-k-(-k+1)=-1

which violates the positivity of
Z(\eta_{1})
. So
\xi_{i}\in E_{\lambda_{0}}
for
i=1,2
.

Now consider
\delta:=\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}
.
\delta
once again satisfies a perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation satisfying the required asymptotic decay conditions, because
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
do. Let
\xi_{\delta}(t)=\lim_{s\rightarrow-\infty}\delta(s,t)
be the associated negative asymptotic eigenvector. We claim that
\xi_{\delta}\in E_{\lambda_{0}}
. Indeed, if not then we may repeat the above argument with
\delta
in the place of
\eta_{1}
and
\xi_{\delta}
in the place of
\xi_{1}
to derive a contradiction. It follows that the map which sends
\eta\in\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u}
to its negative asymptotic eigenvector in
E_{\lambda_{0}}
is linear, and it is injective by Theorem
3.7
(since the negative asymptotic eigenvector of
\eta
is zero if and only if
\eta
vanishes on the negative end, and thus by the Carlemann similarity principle,
\eta
vanishes everywhere). Since
\dim E_{\lambda_{0}}=1
, this proves the claim.
∎

4
Constructing chain-level continuation maps with prescribed behaviour

In this section, we introduce a technique for designing chain-level continuation maps such that certain
0
-dimensional moduli spaces may be guaranteed to be non-empty. The basic idea is to show that, given any collection of smooth cylinders which topologically
could
be a collection of (
s
-dependent) Floer-type cylinders (in the sense that their graphs intersect only positively and they exhibit the appropriate asymptotic winding behaviour), we may — after a series of suitable small perturbations — always find regular Floer homotopy data
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
such that the perturbed cylinders are all
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
-Floer.

For the remainder of the section, we fix Floer regular
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)\times C^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathcal{J}(M,\omega))
.

Definition 4.1
.

We will say that a finite collection of smooth maps
\{u_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma\}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
is a
model for a continuation cobordism
(from
(H^{-},J^{-})
to
(H^{+},J^{+})
) if there exists
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
such that
u_{i}
is an
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
-Floer cylinder with finite energy for each
i=1,\ldots,k
. Such a model for a continuation cobordism will be called an
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
-model
.

If we are doing Floer theory on a surface, then the results of Section
3.2
tell us that the graphs
\tilde{u}_{i}
, when restricted to a sufficiently large compact set
[-K,K]\times S^{1}
, define a braid cobordism having only positive intersections (outside this compact set, the maps
u_{i}
solve the
s
-independent Floer equations on the ends, and their behaviour is controlled by the winding behaviour of the eigenvectors of the asymptotic operator as discussed in Section
3.2
).

Recall (Definition
2.11
) that a braid cobordism
h=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})
is said to be positive if the graphs of its strands are all transverse with positive intersections. The main result of this section is to show that this essentially topological condition, combined with the obvious necessary condition on the asymptotic behaviour of the strands, is sufficient to guarantee the existence of some regular homotopy of Floer data
(\mathcal{H},J)
such that each cylinder is
(\mathcal{H},J)
-Floer.

Definition 4.2
.

We will say that a finite collection of smooth maps
u_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, is a
pre-model for a continuation cobordism
(from
(H^{-},J^{-})
to
(H^{+},J^{+})
) if they satisfy the following

1.

There exists
K>0
such that the maps
u_{i}|_{[-K,K]}
are the strands of a positive braid cobordism, and

2.

for the same
K
as above,
u_{i}|_{(-\infty,-K]}
satisfies the
(H^{-},J^{-})
-Floer equation and has finite
(H^{-},J^{-})
-energy and, while
u_{i}|_{[K,\infty)}
satisfies the
(H^{+},J^{+})
-Floer equation and has finite
(H^{+},J^{+})
energy, for each
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Our aim is to show:

Theorem 4.3
.

If
u_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
,
i=1,\ldots,k
defines a pre-model for a continuation cobordism from
(H^{-},J^{-})
to
(H^{+},J^{+})
, then there exists
u_{i}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
,
i=1,\ldots,k
which is a model for a continuation cobordism from
(H^{-},J^{-})
to
(H^{+},J^{+})
and such that each
u_{i}^{\prime}
differs from
u_{i}
only by a
C^{0}
-small homotopy inside a compact subset of
(-K,K)\times S^{1}
(where here
K>0
is as in the definition of a pre-model for a continuation cobordism).

The principal virtue of this result is that the existence or non-existence of pre-models may largely be reduced to the question of the relative topologies of the collections of capped orbits (
qua
capped braids) being connected by these cylinders, along with asymptotic information provided by the asymptotic operator.

The argument itself is straightforward and rather hands-on. Our goal is to show that given a pre-model for a cobordism from
(H^{-},J^{-})
to
(H^{+},J^{+})
, we may build a homotopy of Floer data
(\mathcal{H},J)\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
such that each
u_{i}
is
(\mathcal{H},J)
-Floer. When

(u_{1}(s_{0},t_{0}),\ldots,u_{k}(s_{0},t_{0}))\in C_{k}(\Sigma)
, it is not difficult to simply choose
(\mathcal{H}^{s_{0}}_{t_{0}},J^{s_{0}}_{t_{0}})
such that each
u_{i}
satisfies the relevant Floer-equation at
(s_{0},t_{0})
, and this assignment may be made smoothly in
(s,t)
as long as
u_{i}(s,t)\neq u_{j}(s,t)
for
i\neq j
. The difficulty, therefore, arises when the graphs of the cylinders intersect. To resolve this, in Section
4.1
we explain how to perturb the cylinders in a neighbourhood
V
of an intersection point to another pre-model which satisfies the Floer equations for a judiciously chosen pair
(\mathcal{H}_{V},J_{V})
on
V
. In Section
4.2
, we prove Theorem
4.3
by performing this perturbation at every intersection point, and then arguing that we may extend the locally judiciously chosen s-dependent Hamiltonian-almost complex structure pairs to an adapted homotopy of Floer data
(\mathcal{H},J)
such that the resulting perturbed cylinders are all
(\mathcal{H},J)
-Floer. Finally, in Section
4.3
, we address the question of obtaining model cobordisms for
regular
homotopies of Floer data from the existence of the model cobordisms constructed in Theorem
4.3
. This gives a method for designing Floer continuation maps which have prescribed chain-level behaviour.

4.1
Perturbing positively intersecting strands to solve Floer’s equation near the intersection

For
\kappa>0
, let
D(\kappa)\subset\mathbb{C}
denote the closed disk of radius
\kappa
, centered at the origin. We write
D=D(1)
for the closed unit disk.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(M^{4},J)
be a smooth almost complex
4
-manifold, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a smooth oriented codimension
2
foliation of
M
such that
T\mathcal{F}
is
J
-invariant. Suppose that
u,v:D\rightarrow M
are smooth embeddings which are positively transverse to the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
and which intersect positively at
u(0)=v(0)=p\in M
. Suppose moreover that
v
is
J
-holomorphic on some neighbourhood of
p
. Then for any
C^{0}
-neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
u
in
C^{\infty}(D;M)
, there exists
\kappa>0
,
\delta>0
and
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
such that

1.

u^{\prime}|_{D(\kappa+\delta)}
intersects
v
only at
p=u^{\prime}(0)
and this intersection is transverse,

2.

u^{\prime}
is
J
-holomorphic on
D(\kappa)
,

3.

u^{\prime}=u
on
D\setminus D(\kappa+\delta)
,

4.

u^{\prime}
is positively transverse to the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

By the local existence theorem for holomorphic curves (see theorem
3.1.1
in
[
48
]
, for instance), there exists a
\rho>0
and a
J
-holomorphic map
f:D(\rho)\rightarrow M
such that
f(0)=p
and
(\partial_{s}f)_{p}=(\partial_{s}u)_{p}
. Up to taking
\rho
to be smaller and restricting the domain of
f
, we may assume that
f
is an embedding. Note that,
(\partial_{s}u)_{p}=(\partial_{s}f)_{p}\neq 0
and hence
(\partial_{t}f)_{p}\neq 0
, so
f
and
v
intersect transversally and positively, since both are
J
-holomorphic. Note that similar reasoning shows that
f
is positively transverse to the leaf of
\mathcal{F}
passing through
p
, and so
f
is positively transverse to
\mathcal{F}
for all
z\in D(\rho)
sufficiently close to
0
. We will construct
u^{\prime}
by interpolating between
u
and
f
. In order to construct this interpolation, it will be useful to introduce a convenient local coordinate system.

To this end, let us note that there is a neighbourhood
U\subset M
of
p
, an
\epsilon>0
, and an orientation-preserving chart

\displaystyle\phi:U

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C},

such that for all
z\in D(\epsilon)
,
(\phi\circ v)(z)=(z,0)
. Moreover, if the foliation
\mathcal{F}
is as in the statement of the lemma, then the fact that
v
intersects positively and transversally with the leaves of
\mathcal{F}
implies that
\phi
may be chosen such that for any leaf
F\in\mathcal{F}
,
\phi(F\cap U)\subset\{z_{0}\}\times\mathbb{C}
for some
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
. That is,
\phi
locally diffeomorphically sends leaves of
\mathcal{F}
into the fibers of the projection map onto the first coordinate. Since
u
and
f
are both positively transverse to
v
, up to shrinking
\epsilon>0
, for
z\in D(\epsilon)
we may write

\displaystyle f_{loc}(z):=(\phi\circ f)(z)=(z,h_{0}(z)),

\displaystyle u_{loc}(z):=(\phi\circ u)(z)=(z,h_{1}(z))

for
h_{0},h_{1}:D(\epsilon)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
smooth functions such that
h_{0}(0)=h_{1}(0)=0
,
(\partial_{s}h_{0})_{0}=(\partial_{s}h_{1})_{0}\neq 0
, and
\{(\partial_{s}h_{i})_{0},(\partial_{t}h_{i})_{0}\}
is a positively-oriented basis for
\mathbb{C}
for
i=0,1
. Next, for
\tau\in[0,1]
, let us define
h_{\tau}:D(\epsilon)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\displaystyle h_{\tau}(z)

\displaystyle:=(1-\tau)h_{0}(z)+\tau h_{1}(z).

Note that
(\partial_{s}h_{\tau})_{0}
is constant in
\tau
, and so it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\{(\partial_{s}h_{\tau})_{0},(\partial_{t}h_{\tau})_{0}\}

\displaystyle=\{(\partial_{s}h_{0})_{0},(1-\tau)(\partial_{t}h_{0})_{0}+\tau(\partial_{t}h_{1})_{0}\}

is positively oriented, as the set of all vectors
w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\{(\partial_{s}h_{0})_{0},w\}
is positively oriented is clearly a convex set and contains both
(\partial_{t}h_{0})_{0}
and
(\partial_{t}h_{1})_{0}
by hypothesis. Consequently, for each
\tau\in[0,1]
there exists
\epsilon_{\tau}\in(0,\epsilon)
, varying continuously with
\tau
, such that
h_{\tau}(z)=0
if and only if
z=0
for all
z\in D(\epsilon_{\tau})
. Posing

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\tau\in[0,1]}\epsilon_{\tau},

we see that
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
and the maps
u_{\tau}(z):=(z,h_{\tau}(z))
intersect
D(\epsilon^{\prime})\times\{0\}
only in
(0,0)
for all
\tau\in[0,1]
. Fix
\kappa\in(0,\epsilon^{\prime})
and some
\delta\in(0,\epsilon^{\prime}-\kappa)
, and let
\beta:D(\epsilon^{\prime})\rightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth radially non-decreasing function which is identically
0
on
D(\kappa)
and identically
1
outside of
D(\kappa+\delta)
. For
z\in D(\epsilon^{\prime})
, we define

\displaystyle u^{\prime}_{loc}(z):=u_{\beta(z)}(z)=(1-\beta(z))f_{loc}+\beta(z)u_{loc}.

Note that
\phi^{-1}\circ u^{\prime}_{loc}
then obviously satisfies the first three items listed in the lemma, by construction, and is moreover transparently positively transverse to leaves of
\mathcal{F}
by construction, since
\phi^{-1}
sends graphs of maps
\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
to submanifolds transverse to leaves of
\mathcal{F}
by our choice of
\phi
. We then obtain the claimed
u^{\prime}:D\rightarrow M
by setting

u^{\prime}(z)=\begin{cases}(\phi^{-1}\circ u^{\prime}_{loc})(z)&z\in D(\epsilon^{\prime})\\
u(z)&z\in D\setminus D(\epsilon^{\prime}).\end{cases}

(6)

It is then clear from construction that
u^{\prime}
satisfies the four properties listed in the lemma. Moreover, because
f(0)=u(0)=p
and
f
and
u
are both continuous,
u^{\prime}
may obviously be taken to be as
C^{0}
-close to
u
as we wish, simply by taking
\kappa
and
\delta
smaller in the preceding argument if necessary.
∎

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
u,v:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
be smooth maps such that their graphs
\tilde{u},\tilde{v}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma
intersect positively and transversally at
(s_{0},t_{0},p)\in\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma
. Then for any neighbourhood
V\subset\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
of
(s_{0},t_{0})
, there exists an open set
U\subset\bar{U}\subset V
containing
(s_{0},t_{0})
, smooth maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\bar{U}}:\bar{U}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle J_{\bar{U}}:\bar{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega),

and a smooth map
u^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
agreeing with
u
outside of
V
, such that
\tilde{u}^{\prime}
intersects
\tilde{v}
positively and transversally at
(s_{0},t_{0},p)
, and such that both
u^{\prime}
and
v
solve the
(\mathcal{H}_{\bar{U}},J_{\bar{U}})
-Floer equation for
(s,t)\in\bar{U}
.

Proof.

All that needs to be shown here is that for
V
a small enough neighbourhood of
(s_{0},t_{0})
, we may always choose
\mathcal{H}_{V}:V\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
J_{V}:V\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega)
such that
v
is
(\mathcal{H}_{V},J_{V})
-Floer on
V
. Once this is established, we simply apply Lemma
4.4
to
\tilde{u}
and
\tilde{v}
after using Gromov’s trick to construct an almost complex structure
\tilde{J}^{\mathcal{H}}
on
V\times\Sigma
such that maps solve the
(\mathcal{H}_{V},J_{V})
-Floer equations if and only if their graphs are
\tilde{J}^{\mathcal{H}}
-holomorphic over
V
. Recall from Section
3.1.3
that the fibers of the projection map
pr_{cyl}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
are pseudo-holomorphic for the almost complex structure given by the Gromov trick, and so Lemma
4.4
gives us an open set
U\subset\bar{U}\subset V
containing
(s_{0},t_{0})
and a map
\tilde{u}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma
which is transverse to
\tilde{v}
on
U
and which is transverse to the fibers of
pr_{cyl}
— and thus
\tilde{u}^{\prime}
is the graph of some function
u^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
which is
\tilde{J}^{\mathcal{H}}
-holomorphic on
\bar{U}
, and
\tilde{u}^{\prime}
agrees with
\tilde{u}
outside of
V
. We then simply take
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{U}}:=\mathcal{H}_{V}|_{\bar{U}}
,
J_{\bar{U}}:=J_{V}|_{\bar{U}}
.

To see that
\mathcal{H}_{V}:V\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
J_{V}:V\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega)
may be chosen as claimed, let
J_{V}
be arbitrary, and define

\displaystyle X:V

\displaystyle\rightarrow v^{*}T\Sigma|_{V}

\displaystyle(s,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\partial_{t}v-J_{V}\partial_{s}v)_{(s,t)}.

It’s straightforward to see that we may choose
\mathcal{H}_{V}
to satisfy
X^{\mathcal{H}_{V}}(v(s,t))=X(s,t)
for all
(s,t)\in V
. Indeed, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(X)

\displaystyle:=\{(s,t,H)\in V\times C^{\infty}(\Sigma):X^{H}(v(s,t))=X(s,t)\}.

It is not difficult to verify that
\pi:\mathcal{H}(X)\rightarrow V
is a locally trivial fibration (see Corollary
7.2
in Appendix
7.1
), with
\pi
being the obvious projection map. The fiber over
z\in V
is diffeomorphic to the subspace of functions on
\Sigma
having a critical point at
v(z)
. This shows that the fibers are contractible, and so there exists a section
\mathcal{H}_{V}
. By construction,
v
is
(\mathcal{H}_{V},J_{V})
-Floer on
V
.
∎

4.2
Proof of Theorem
4.3

Let us now prove Theorem
4.3
.

Proof.

We first note that if
\{u_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma\}_{i=1}^{k}
, is a pre-model for a continuation cobordism, then

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=\{(s,t)\in[-K,K]\times S^{1}:u_{i}(s,t)=u_{j}(s,t),1\leq i<j\leq k\},

where
K
is as in the definition of a pre-model for a continuation cobordism, contains only finitely many points and lies in the interior of
[-K,K]\times S^{1}
. By Corollary
4.5
for each
z\in I
, there exists an arbitrarily small open set
U_{z}
containing
z
, local sections

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{z}_{loc}:\bar{U}_{z}

\displaystyle\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle J^{z}_{loc}:\bar{U}_{z}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega),

and small perturbations
u_{i}^{\prime}
of each
u_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
such that each
u_{i}^{\prime}
satisfies the
(\mathcal{H}^{z}_{loc},J^{z}_{loc})
-Floer equation on
\bar{U}_{z}
. Let us write

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle:=([-K,K]\times S^{1})\setminus\bigcup_{z\in I}U_{z}.

Our goal is to find smooth maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{V}:V

\displaystyle\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle J_{V}:V

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega),

which agree with
\mathcal{H}^{z}_{loc}
and
J^{z}_{loc}
respectively on
\partial\bar{U}_{z}
, which in addition agree with
H^{\pm}
and with
J^{\pm}
respectively on
\{\pm K\}\times S^{1}
, and such that, moreover, each
u_{i}^{\prime}
is
(\mathcal{H}_{V},J_{V})
-Floer on
V
. Clearly, if this can be done, these data will patch together to give a homotopy
(\mathcal{H},J)
of Floer data between
(H^{-},J^{-})
and
(H^{+},J^{+})
such that each
u_{i}^{\prime}
is
(\mathcal{H},J)
-Floer on all of
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
, and we will be done.

To see that
(\mathcal{H}_{V},J_{V})
may be chosen as desired we argue essentially identically to the proof of Corollary
4.5
. First take
J_{V}
to be any smooth map
J_{V}:V\rightarrow\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega)
which restricts to the desired almost complex structures on
\partial V
. Clearly, this can be done, since the space of
\omega
-compatible almost complex structures is contractible. Next, for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, we define a section of
(u_{i}^{\prime}|_{V})^{*}T\Sigma
by

\displaystyle X_{i}:V

\displaystyle\mapsto(u_{i}^{\prime}|_{V})^{*}T\Sigma

\displaystyle(s,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\partial_{t}u_{i}^{\prime}-J_{V}\partial_{s}u_{i}^{\prime})_{(s,t)},

and we define
\mathcal{H}(\vec{X})\rightarrow V
to be the fiber bundle with fiber

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\vec{X})_{s,t}

\displaystyle:=\{H\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma):X^{H}(u_{i}^{\prime}(s,t))=X_{i}(s,t),\;\forall(s,t)\in V,\;\forall i=1,\ldots,k\}.

It is not difficult to see that
\mathcal{H}(\vec{X})
is a locally trivial fibration (apply Corollary
7.2
from Appendix
7.1
in the setting where the underlying symplectic manifold is the configuration space
C_{k}(\Sigma)
) and that each
\mathcal{H}^{z}_{loc}|_{\partial\bar{U}_{z}}
defines a section of
\mathcal{H}(\vec{X})
over
\partial\bar{U}_{z}
, in addition to
(\pm K,t)\mapsto H^{\pm}_{t}
defining a section over
\{\pm K\}\times S^{1}
. Moreover, each fiber of
\mathcal{H}(\vec{X})
is diffeomorphic to the space of functions on
\Sigma
having critical points at
k
distinct points. In particular,
\mathcal{H}(\vec{X})
has contractible fibers and therefore admits a smooth section extending the given aforementioned data on
\partial V
, completing the proof.
∎

4.3
From models of cobordisms to regular models

Our ultimate goal is to use the topological construction of pre-models for cobordisms to guarantee the more rigid existence of Floer continuation maps with prescribed behaviour. As such, we will need to consider issues of regularity.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be Floer regular. A model for a continuation cobordism from
(H^{-},J^{-})
to
(H^{+},J^{+})
will be called
regular
if it is an
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
-model for some

(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}_{reg}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
.

The following lemma follows essentially by combining Corollaries
3.23
and
3.24
with results which are likely folkloric: that local transversality of the
s
-dependent Floer operator at some cylinder
u
only requires perturbations with support contained in an appropriately dense open neighbourhood of the graph of
u
, and the fact that near a minimally degenerate Floer cylinder of index
0
(ie. when
\dim\ker(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u})=1
), the universal moduli space locally has the structure of a ‘fold’ singularity over the space of perturbation data. I am unaware of a place in the published literature where such results are stated, so for the convenience of the reader, a detailed proof sketch is provided in Appendix
7.1
(see Section
7.1.2
).

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be Floer-regular,
(\mathcal{H},J)\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
,
\hat{x}^{\pm}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H^{\pm}})
, and
u\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)_{\hat{x}^{-},\hat{x}^{+}}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},J}(u)=0
and that
ind\;(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},J})_{u}=0
. Let
N\subset\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma
denote a neighbourhood of
\text{im}\;\tilde{u}
and let
U\subset N
be an open dense subset of
N
such that
U\cap\text{im}\;\tilde{u}
is dense in
\text{im}\;\tilde{u}
. For any neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)_{\hat{x}-,\hat{x}^{+}}
, there exists
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{H}(H^{-};H^{+})
and
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
such that
\text{supp }\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\subset U
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime},J}(u^{\prime})=0
, and
(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime},J})_{u^{\prime}}
is surjective.

With this lemma in hand, we can readily prove:

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be Floer regular and let
u_{i}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
,
i=1,\ldots,k
define an
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
-model for a continuation cobordism for some
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
. Suppose that for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, the index of the linearized Floer operator satisfies

\displaystyle ind\;(D\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J}})_{u_{i}}

\displaystyle=0,

then for any choice of neighbourhoods
\mathcal{U}_{i}
of
u_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)_{\hat{x}_{i},\hat{y}_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, there exists a regular model for a continuation cobordism
\{u_{i}^{\prime\prime}\}_{i=1}^{k}
such that
u_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Proof.

For each
i=1,\ldots,k
, let
\mathcal{N}(\text{im}\;\tilde{u}_{i})
be an open neighbourhood of
\text{im}\;\tilde{u}_{i}
and let

\displaystyle U_{i}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{N}(\text{im}\;\tilde{u}_{i})\setminus\bigcup_{j\neq i}\text{im}\;\tilde{u}_{i}.

Since graphs
\tilde{u}_{i},\tilde{u}_{j}
have only finitely many intersections for
i\neq j
,
U_{i}
clearly satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
4.7
with
u=u_{i}
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
. It follows that there exists
h_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma)
with compact support contained in
U_{i}
and
u_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
such that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}+h_{i},J}(u_{i}^{\prime})=0
, and
u_{i}^{\prime}
is a regular point of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}+h_{i},J}
. Note that, because the support of
h_{i}
is compactly contained in
U_{i}
which is disjoint from
\text{im}\;\tilde{u}_{j}
for each
j\neq i
, there exists an open neighbourhood
V_{j}
of
\text{im}\;\tilde{u}_{j}
such
\text{supp }h_{i}\cap V_{j}=\emptyset
. Consequently, since the local behaviour of the Floer operator near a map depends only on the behaviour of the continuation data near the graph of that map, if we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}

we have that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime},J}(u^{\prime}_{i})=0
for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, and each
u_{i}^{\prime}
is a regular point of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime},J}
. Finally, in light of the regularity of each
u_{i}^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime},J}
and the index condition on the Floer operator, we may perturb
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
to some
\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{H}(H^{-},H^{+})
such that
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime},J)\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}_{reg}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
, such that there exist maps
u_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
with
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime},J}(u_{i}^{\prime\prime})=0
for
i=1,\ldots,k
. This completes the proof.
∎

As an easy consequence of the previous theorem, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
(H^{\pm},J^{\pm})
be Floer regular, and let
\hat{x}^{\pm}_{i}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H^{\pm}})
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, be such that
\mu(\hat{x}_{i}^{-})=\mu(\hat{x}_{i}^{+})
. If there exists a model for a continuation cobordism
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
with
u_{i}\in C^{\infty}_{\hat{x}^{-}_{i},\hat{x}^{+}_{i}}(\mathbb{R}\times S^{1};\Sigma)
, then there exists
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}_{reg}(H^{-},J^{-};H^{+},J^{+})
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\hat{x}_{i}^{-},\hat{x}^{+}_{i};\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})

\displaystyle\neq\emptyset

for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

5
Application: A new spectral invariant and its computation on surfaces

In this section, we bring to bear the theory developed in the preceding pages in order to give an explicit dynamical characterization — for any non-degenerate Hamiltonian
H
on a symplectic surface
(\Sigma,\omega)
— of those Floer cycles which both represent a non-trivial homology class in
HF_{1}(H)

and
which lie in the image of some PSS map at the chain level.

This dynamical characterization motivates us to study the quantity obtained by modifying the definition of the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants by looking at the minimal action required to represent a given homology class by a cycle which lies in the image of some PSS map at the chain level. It turns out that this defines a family of action selectors, which we call the
PSS-image spectral invariants
. These novel spectral invariants share many of the same properties as the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants, including a triangle inequality for the fundamental class, and bound the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants from above.

The section begins with a general study of the PSS-image spectral invariants in Section
5.1
, establishing their basic formal properties, whose proofs are largely analogous to the corresponding proofs for the the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants. In Section
5.2
, we compute the PSS-image spectral invariant of a non-degenerate Hamiltonian on a surface by deriving the aforementioned dynamical characterization of Floer cycles which represent the fundamental class and which lie in the image of some PSS map at the chain level.

The main line of the proof of Theorem
C
is as follows. The fact that the cycle in question lies in the image of some chain-level PSS map implies the existence of various PSS disks, which may be thought of as providing a deformation from a trivial capped braid to the support of the cycle in question. The theory from Sections
2
and
3
may then be used to force the support of this cycle to be positive, and when combined with the cycle’s homological non-triviality this theory forces the support to be
maximally
positive (relative index
1
). Conversely, in order to deduce that
every
such capped braid lies in the image of some chain level PSS map, it is sufficient to construct an appropriate continuation map from a small Morse function which sends the fundamental class of the Morse function to a cycle supported on the capped braid in question. The technique for designing continuation maps with prescribed behaviour developed in Section
4
may be seen to provide precisely such a continuation map.

Section
5.3
presents two fairly immediate dynamical consequences of this characterization, and the section closes with a brief discussion of the relationship between the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants and the PSS-image spectral invariants in Section
5.4
.

5.1
A new spectral invariant: definitions and properties

Let
(H,J)
be Floer-regular. Recall that the set
PSS(H,J)
of
PSS data
for
(H,J)
is the collection of tuples

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}=(f,g;\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in C^{\infty}(M)\times Met(M)\times\mathscr{H}(0;H)\times\mathcal{J}^{PSS}(J),

where
(f,g)
is a Morse-Smale pair. There is a residual set
PSS_{reg}(H,J)
of
regular PSS data
such that for any
\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{reg}^{PSS}(H,J)
, we may define a chain-level PSS map
\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}:QC_{*+n}(f,g)\rightarrow CF_{*}(H,J)
which is
\Lambda_{\omega}
-linear and induces a natural isomorphism at the level of homology.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(H,J)
be Floer-regular. For
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M,\omega)
,
\alpha\neq 0
, we define the
PSS-image spectral invariant

\displaystyle c_{im}(\alpha;H,J)

\displaystyle=c_{im}(\alpha;H)

\displaystyle:=\inf_{\mathcal{D}\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)}\{\lambda_{H}(\sigma):\sigma\in\text{im}\;\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}^{PSS},\;\text{and}\;[\sigma]=(\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}^{PSS})_{*}\alpha\}.

In general, it is not immediately obvious that
c_{im}(\alpha;H)\neq-\infty
for all
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M,\omega)\setminus\{0\}
. To see that this is so, recall the definition of the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariant
c_{OS}(\alpha;H)
for
0\neq\alpha\in QH_{*}(M)
from Section
3.1.2
. These spectral invariants are always finite (see theorem 5.3 in
[
35
]
, for instance). Moreover, it is an obvious consequence of the definitions that we have:

Proposition 5.2
.

For any
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M)\setminus\{0\}
and any
J\in\mathcal{J}_{\omega}(M)
such that
(H,J)
is Floer regular

\displaystyle c_{OS}(\alpha;H)

\displaystyle\leq c_{im}(\alpha;H,J).

Consequently, we obtain the finiteness of
c_{im}(\alpha;H,J)
for all non-zero quantum homology classes
\alpha
.

5.1.1
Basic Properties of
c_{im}

We describe now some of the basic properties of the PSS-image invariants. The proofs are essentially routine adaptations of the corresponding proofs for the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants (see for example
[
35
]
), requiring only that one additionally exhibits some family of chain-level PSS maps which realizes the infimizing process defining
c_{PSS}(\alpha;H,J)
. As such, we omit most of the proofs and defer some illustrative ones to Appendix
7.2
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
(H,J)
,
(K,J^{\prime})
be Floer regular pairs. For any
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M,\omega)
,
\alpha\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\min_{x\in M}(K-H)(t,x)\;dt\leq c_{im}(\alpha;K,J^{\prime})

\displaystyle-c_{im}(\alpha;H,J)\leq\int_{0}^{1}\max_{x\in M}(K-H)(t,x)\;dt

Corollary 5.4
.

c_{im}(\alpha;H)=c_{im}(\alpha;H,J)
is well-defined and independent of the choice of regular
J
.

We defer the proof of the following to Appendix
7.2
(Section
7.2
).

Proposition 5.5
.

For any
H,K\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)
and any
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M,\omega)\setminus\{0\}

1.

if
r:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is smooth, then

\displaystyle c_{im}(\alpha;H+r)

\displaystyle=c_{im}(\alpha;H)+\int_{0}^{1}r(t)\;dt.

2.

c_{im}(\psi_{*}\alpha;\psi_{*}H)=c_{im}(\alpha;H)
for any symplectic diffeomorphism
\psi
.

3.

|c_{im}(\alpha;H)-c_{im}(\alpha;K)|\leq\|H-K\|_{L^{1,\infty}}
.

4.

(Weak triangle inequality)
c_{im}(\alpha;H\#K)\leq c_{im}(\alpha;H)+c_{im}([M];K)
.

The above proposition implies that
c_{im}(\alpha;H)
is
C^{0}
-continuous in its Hamiltonian argument. By the density of non-degenerate Hamiltonians in the space of all Hamiltonians, we may extend the definition of
c(\alpha;H)
to any
H\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)
. The above properties then obviously still hold by approximation even if the Hamiltonians in question are not presumed non-degenerate.

Moreover, if we define, for
\alpha=\sum\alpha_{A}e^{A}\in QH_{*}(M)
its
valuation

\displaystyle\nu(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\max\{-\omega(A):\alpha_{A}\neq 0\},

then we see that the PSS-image spectral invariants satisfy the same normalization condition as the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants.

Proposition 5.6
.

For
\alpha=\sum\alpha_{A}e^{A}\in QH_{*}(M)\setminus\{0\}
, we have

\displaystyle c_{im}(\alpha;0)

\displaystyle=\nu(\alpha).

The proof is identical to the one for the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariants.

Corollary 5.7
.

For any
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M,\omega)
,
\alpha\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\min_{x\in M}H(t,x)\;dt+\nu(\alpha)\leq c_{im}(\alpha;H)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\max_{x\in M}H(t,x)\;dt+\nu(\alpha).

In particular, if
\alpha\in H_{*}(M)
, then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\min_{x\in M}H_{t}\;dt\leq c_{im}(\alpha;H)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\max_{x\in M}H_{t}\;dt

The PSS-image spectral invariant also shares the following properties of the Oh-Schwarz spectral invariant, whose proofs are once more identical to those for the latter. We refer the interested reader to Theorem II and its proof in
[
35
]
.

Proposition 5.8
.

If
(M,\omega)
is rational in the sense that the subgroup
\omega(\pi_{2}(M))\subset\mathbb{R}
is discrete, then

1.

\displaystyle c_{im}(\alpha;H)

\displaystyle\in Spec(H)

for all
\alpha\in QH_{*}(M,\omega)\setminus\{0\}
.

2.

If
H\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)
is assumed to be normalized so that
\int H_{t}\omega^{n}=0
for all
t\in S^{1}
, then
c_{im}(\alpha;H)
depends only on the homotopy class of the isotopy
(\phi^{H}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
relative endpoints.

5.1.2
A new spectral norm

The fact that the PSS-image spectral invariants satisfy the triangle inequality for the fundamental class allows us to define an associated symplectic conjugation invariant pseudo-norm on
\widetilde{Ham}(M,\omega)
which descends to a non-degenerate conjugation invariant norm on
Ham(M,\omega)
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a compact rational semi-positive symplectic manifold. We define the function

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{im}:C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\mapsto c_{im}([M];H)+c_{im}([M];\bar{H}),

which descends to the
PSS-image pseudo-norm
on
\widetilde{Ham}(M,\omega)
by defining

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{im}:\widetilde{Ham}(M,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}^{H}

\displaystyle\mapsto c_{im}([M];H)+c_{im}([M];\bar{H}),

where
\tilde{\phi}^{H}
denotes the homotopy class relative endpoints in
Ham(M,\omega)
of the path
(\phi^{H}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
H\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)
, then

1.

\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(H)=\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(\bar{H})
,

2.

\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(H\#F)\leq\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(H)+\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(F)
,
\forall H,F\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times M)
. In particular

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(H).

3.

\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(H)\leq\int_{0}^{1}\max_{x\in M}H_{t}-\min_{x\in M}H_{t}\;dt

Definition 5.11
.

To obtain a norm on the group of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, we define the
PSS-image spectral norm

\displaystyle\gamma_{im}:Ham(M,\omega)

\displaystyle\rightarrow[0,\infty)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\mapsto\inf_{\tilde{\phi}}\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(\tilde{\phi}),

where the infimum is taken over all
\tilde{\phi}\in\widetilde{Ham}(M,\omega)
which project to
\phi\in Ham(M,\omega)
under the natural projection
\widetilde{Ham}(M,\omega)\rightarrow Ham(M,\omega)
.

Proposition 5.12
.

\gamma_{im}
is a norm. Moreover, for all
\phi\in Ham(M,\omega)
,

\displaystyle\gamma_{OS}(\phi)\leq\gamma_{im}

\displaystyle(\phi)\leq\|\phi\|_{Hof},

where
\gamma_{OS}
denotes the Oh-Schwarz spectral norm and
\|\cdot\|_{Hof}
denotes the Hofer norm.

5.2
Computing
c_{im}([\Sigma];H)
on surfaces

In this section, we restrict our attention to the case of
M=\Sigma
an arbitrary closed orientable surface, and we use the theory developed in Sections
2
-
4
to give a topological characterization of all homologically non-trivial
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-cycles in
CF_{1}(H,J)
which lie in the image of some PSS-map. Specifically, we prove:

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
\sigma\in CF_{1}(H,J)
.
\sigma
is a non-trivial cycle with
\sigma\in\text{im}\;\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}
for some
\mathcal{D}\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)
if and only if
\text{supp }\sigma
is a maximal positive capped braid relative index
1
.

Remark 9
.

The dependence of spectral invariants (and so
a fortiori
, of cycles representing the fundamental class) on the choices of coefficients for Floer and quantum homology is a matter of active interest. Since we have restricted our presentation to
\mathbb{Z}/2
-Novikov coefficients in order to avoid dealing with orientations, the above theorem is not particularly informative on this issue. However, for those interested in such questions we will note that the proof of Theorem
5.13
actually implies the following with coefficients in any Novikov ring: If
\sigma\in CF_{1}(H,J)
and
\sigma\in\text{im}\;\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}
for some
\mathcal{D}\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)
, then
\text{supp }\sigma
is a maximal positive capped braid relative index
1
. On the other hand, if
\hat{X}\in mp_{(1)}(H)
, then there exist choices
a_{\hat{x}}\in\{-1,1\}
for
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}
such that
\sum_{\hat{x}\in\hat{X}}a_{\hat{x}}\hat{x}
is a cycle representing the fundamental class in
HF_{*}(H)
and lying in the image of some regular PSS-map. Note that because the level of a Floer chain depends only on its support, this suffices to determine the PSS-image spectral invariant
c_{im}([\Sigma],-)
on surfaces with arbitrary Novikov coefficients (and in fact, it determines
c_{im}([\Sigma];-)
for any suitable choice of Novikov coefficients, and shows moreover that its value is
independent
of this choice of coefficients).

Let us spell out the topological condition that is used in the above characterization. Recall from Definition
2.18
that a capped braid is positive if there exists a
0
-cobordism
h
from a trivially capped braid to
\hat{X}
such that the graphs of the strands of
h
are transverse with any intersections occurring positively. We remind the reader that for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})_{(k)}
denotes the collection of capped
1
-periodic orbits of
H
having Conley-Zehnder index
k
.

Definition 5.14
.

For
H\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times\Sigma)
non-degenerate, a collection of capped
1
-periodic orbits
\hat{X}\subseteq\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})
will be said to be
maximally positive
(resp.
negative
)
relative index
1
(resp.
relative index
-1
) if

1.

\hat{X}\subseteq\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})_{(1)}
(resp.
\hat{X}\subseteq\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})_{(-1)}
) ,

2.

\hat{X}
is a positive (resp. negative) capped braid, and

3.

\hat{X}
is maximal among all subsets of
\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})
satisfying the two previous items.

We denote by
mp_{(1)}(H)
(resp.
mn_{(-1)}(H)
) the set of all such capped braids.

This leads immediately to the following characterization of
c_{im}([\Sigma];-)
in the non-degenerate case.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
H
be non-degenerate.

\displaystyle c_{im}([\Sigma];H)

\displaystyle=\min_{\hat{X}\in mp_{(1)}(H)}\max_{\hat{x}\in\hat{X}}\mathcal{A}_{H}(\hat{x})

We begin with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in PSS(H,J)
, and let
u_{i}:D^{2}\rightarrow\Sigma
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, solve the PSS-equation induced by
\mathcal{D}
. Suppose that for some
(s_{0},t_{0})\in D^{2}
and some
p\in\Sigma
,

\displaystyle u_{i}(s_{0},t_{0})

\displaystyle=p,\;\text{for all }i=1,\ldots,k.

Then there exists an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq D^{2}
of
(s_{0},t_{0})
and smooth maps
v_{i}:D^{2}\rightarrow\Sigma
,
i=1,\ldots,k
such that

1.

v_{i}(s,t)=u_{i}(s,t)
for all
(s,t)\not\in U
,

2.

v_{i}(s_{0},t_{0})\neq p
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
, and

3.

any pairwise intersections of the graphs
\tilde{v}_{i}
and
\tilde{v}_{j}
,
1\leq i<j\leq k
, are both transverse and positive.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the intersection theory for pseudoholomorphic curves in
4
-dimensional almost complex manifolds as worked out in Appendix E of
[
31
]
. In slightly more detail, the proof of Proposition E.2.2 of
[
31
]
shows that if
f:(\Sigma,j)\rightarrow(M,J)
is any simple pseudoholomorphic map with
(\Sigma,j)
any Riemannian surface and
(M,J)
any
4
-dimensional almost complex manifold, then for any self -intersection point
(z_{0},z_{1})\in\Sigma\times\Sigma\setminus\Delta
with
f(z_{0})=f(z_{1})
, there exist (disjoint) neighbourhoods
U_{i}\subseteq\Sigma
of
z_{i}
,
i=0,1
, and a perturbation
f^{\prime}
of
f
differing from
f
only on
U_{0}\bigcup U_{1}
such that
f^{\prime}
has only positive and transverse self-intersections for
(z_{0}^{\prime},z_{1}^{\prime})\in U_{0}\times U_{1}
. Noting that for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, the graph
\tilde{u}_{i}
is
\tilde{J}^{\mathcal{D}}
-holomorphic, where
\tilde{J}^{\mathcal{D}}
is the almost complex structure on
D^{2}\times\Sigma
associated to the PSS data
\mathcal{D}
by the Gromov trick, we may apply this proposition with
f=\sqcup_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{u}_{i}
. The perturbation may be chosen small enough such that each component of
f^{\prime}=\sqcup\tilde{v}_{i}
is still graphical, and without loss of generality (up to another perturbation of
f^{\prime}
), we may suppose that self-intersections of
f^{\prime}
do not occur at
(s_{0},t_{0},p)\in D^{2}\times\Sigma
. Setting
v_{i}:=\pi_{\Sigma}\circ\tilde{v}_{i}
provides the desired maps.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(f,g;\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)
and suppose that
\sigma\in\text{im}\;\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}\subseteq CF_{1}(H,J)
. Then
\text{supp }\sigma
is a positive capped braid.

Proof.

Write
\hat{X}=\{\hat{x}_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{k}\}=\text{supp }\sigma
. Because
\sigma\in\text{im}\;\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}
for each
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}
, there exists
p\in Crit(f)
such that
\mathcal{M}(p,\hat{x};\mathcal{D})\neq\emptyset
. For
i=1,\ldots,k
, we choose
u_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(p_{i},\hat{x}_{i};\mathcal{D})
. Owing to the Gromov trick, and positivity of intersections in dimension
4
, the algebraic intersection number of the graphs
\tilde{u}_{i}
and
\tilde{u}_{j}
is positive for any
1\leq i<j\leq k
. Moreover, using Lemma
5.16
, we may suppose without loss of generality that none of the graphs intersect over
0\in D^{2}
. We observe next that no two PSS-disks are asymptotic as
s\rightarrow\infty
to the same orbit (that is,
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
for
i\neq j
). Indeed, if we set
v^{i}(s,t)=u_{i}(se^{2\pi{\mathrm{i}}t})
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
, then for
i\neq j
we have by Lemma
3.13
that

\displaystyle 0=\ell(p_{i},p_{j})=

\displaystyle\ell_{-\infty}(u_{i},u_{j})\leq\ell_{\infty}(u_{i},u_{j}),

and if
x_{i}=x_{j}
, then
\ell_{\infty}(u_{i},u_{j})\leq a(\hat{x}_{i})
by Corollary
3.17
, where
\hat{x}_{i}
is the unique capping of
x_{i}
such that
\mu_{CZ}(\hat{x}_{i})=1
, and so Lemma
3.20
implies that
a(\hat{x}_{i})=-1
, a contradiction.

As a consequence, the map
s\mapsto(v^{1}(s,\cdot),\ldots,v^{k}(s,\cdot))\in\mathcal{L}_{0}(\Sigma)^{k}
defines a positive
0
-cobordism from the trivial capped braid
(\widehat{u_{1}(0)},\ldots,\widehat{u_{k}(0)})
to the capped braid
\hat{X}
, showing that
\hat{X}
is a positive capped braid.
∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
\mathcal{D}\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)
and suppose that
\sigma\in CF_{1}(H,J)
is such that
\text{supp }\sigma
is a positive capped braid. If
\sigma
is a cycle satisfying
(\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}^{PSS})_{*}([\Sigma])=[\sigma]
, then
\text{supp }\sigma
is maximally positive relative index
1
.

Proof.

We argue by contradiction. Suppose that
(\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}^{PSS})_{*}([\Sigma])=[\sigma]
, but that
\hat{X}=\text{supp }\sigma
is not maximally positive relative index
1
. Then there must exist
\hat{y}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}(H)_{(1)}
with
\ell(\hat{x},\hat{y})\geq 0
for all
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}
, but
\hat{y}\not\in\hat{X}
. Choose
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
to be a
C^{2}
-small Morse function with a unique maximum
M\in\Sigma
, and let
J^{+}\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma,\omega)
be such that
(f,J^{+})
is Floer-regular. Here, the
C^{2}
-smallness of
f
is taken to be such that such that
CF_{*}(f,J^{+})
may be canonically identified with
QC_{*+1}(f,g_{J^{+}})
, and the fact that
f
has a unique maximum at
M
then implies that
CF_{1}(f,J^{+})
is generated by the constant orbit based at
M
, equipped with the constant capping.

Let
u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\rightarrow\Sigma
be a smooth cylinder such that
u(s,t)=y(t)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently small, and
u(s,t)=M
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently large.
u
is obviously a pre-model for a continuation cobordism, and so by Theorem
4.3
, there exists a homotopy of Floer data
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(H,J;f,J^{+})
such that
u
solves the
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
Floer equation and so in particular defines a
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
-model as in Definition
4.1
(strictly speaking, Theorem
4.3
states that this is true up to a small perturbation of
u
, but if one works through the proof in the case of a single cylinder, one sees that this perturbation is not necessary. Using
u
is mainly a matter of notational convenience). By Corollary
3.23
,
u
is regular, and so up to perturbing
(\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})
to a regular pair
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
, and perturbing
u
to some
u^{\prime}
which solves the
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
-Floer equation if necessary, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\hat{y},\hat{M};\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\neq\emptyset.

Consequently, for all
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}
, we must have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\hat{x},\hat{M};\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

for if
v\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{x},\hat{M};\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
, then we must have

\displaystyle 0\leq\ell(\hat{x},\hat{y})=

\displaystyle\ell_{-\infty}(u^{\prime},v)\leq\ell_{\infty}(u^{\prime},v)\leq a(\hat{M})=-1,

which is a contradiction. Consequently,
h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}(\sigma)=0
, but this is absurd, since
\sigma
represents a non-trivial homology class in
CF_{*}(H,J)
, and so
h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}(\sigma)\neq 0
, since
h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism on homology. It follows that no
\hat{y}
as above may exist and hence
\hat{X}\in mp_{(1)}(H)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.13
.

Corollary
5.17
together with Proposition
5.18
immediately implies that the support of every cycle representing the fundamental class and lying in the image of some PSS map must be a maximal positive braid relative index
1
. Let us show the converse. Let
\hat{X}=\{\hat{x}_{1},\ldots,\hat{x}_{k}\}\subseteq\widetilde{Per}_{0}({H})_{(1)}
be maximally positive relative index
1
, and fix some
J\in C^{\infty}(S^{1};\mathcal{J}_{\omega}(\Sigma))
such that
(H,J)
is Floer regular. We will show that there exists
\mathcal{D}=(f,g;\mathcal{H},\mathbb{J})\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)
such that
f
is a Morse function and

\displaystyle\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in Crit(f)\\
\mu^{Morse}(M)=2\end{subarray}}M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{x}\in\hat{X}}\hat{x}.

To do this, note first that it will suffice to show that there exists some Floer regular pair
(f^{\prime},J^{-})
with
f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
a
C^{2}
-small Morse function and a regular homotopy of Floer data
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(f^{\prime},J^{-};H,J)
such that

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}(\sum_{\hat{p}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({f^{\prime}})_{(1)}}\hat{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{x}\in\hat{X}}\hat{x}.

Indeed, if
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
as above exists, then because
\sum_{\hat{p}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({f^{\prime}})_{(1)}}\hat{p}
represents the unique non-trivial cycle in
CF_{1}(f^{\prime},J^{-})
,
any
choice of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in PSS^{reg}(f^{\prime},J^{-})
will satisfy

\displaystyle\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in Crit(f)\\
\mu^{Morse}(M)=2\end{subarray}}M)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\hat{p}\in\widetilde{Per}_{0}({f^{\prime}})_{(1)}}\hat{p}

at the chain level, and using the gluing results of
[
42
]
, we may define
\mathcal{D}\in PSS_{reg}(H,J)
as an appropriate gluing
\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\#(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
such that at the chain level, we have

\displaystyle\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}}

\displaystyle=h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}\circ\Phi^{PSS}_{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}}.

With the above understood, note next that since
\hat{X}
is a positive braid, there exists a positive
0
-cobordism
s\mapsto(v^{1}_{s},\ldots,v^{k}_{s})\in\mathcal{L}_{0}(\Sigma)^{k}
from some trivial braid
(\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{k})
to
\hat{X}
, where
\hat{p}_{i}
denotes the trivially capped constant loop based at
p_{i}
. We choose
f^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
to be any
C^{2}
-small Morse function having local maxima precisely at the points
p_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,k
. Let
J^{-}\in\mathcal{J}_{\omega}(\Sigma)
be any compatible almost complex structure such that
(f^{\prime},J^{-})
is Floer regular. Next, we define a pre-model for a continuation cobordism from
(f^{\prime},J^{-})
to
(H,J)
. Fix some
K>0
and for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, define

u_{i}(s,t):=\begin{cases}p_{i}&s\in(-\infty,-K]\\
v^{i}_{s+K}(t)&s\in(-K,-K+1)\\
x_{i}(t)&s\in[K+1,\infty).\end{cases}

It is not hard to verify that
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
defines a pre-model for a continuation cobordism from
(f^{\prime},J^{-})
to
(H,J)
. Theorem
4.3
implies that we may perturb this to a continuation cobordism
\{u_{i}^{\prime}\}_{i=1}^{k}
for some
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})\in\mathscr{H\mspace{-8mu}J}(f^{\prime},J^{-};H,J)
. Since each
u_{i}^{\prime}
is asymptotic at both ends to a non-degenerate orbit of Conley-Zehnder index
1
, Corollary
3.23
implies that without loss of generality, we may take
(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
to be regular. We claim that for each
1\leq i,j\leq k
,

|\mathcal{M}(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{x}_{j};\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})|=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }i\neq j\\
1&\text{if }i=j\end{cases}.

The latter case is guaranteed by Corollary
3.21
together with the existence of
u_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{x}_{i};\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
. To establish the former case, note that since
\{\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{k}\}
is a trivial capped braid, we certainly have
\ell(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{p}_{j})=0
for all
i\neq j
. Suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
v\in\mathcal{M}(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{x}_{j};\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathbb{J}^{\prime})
. Then we must have

\displaystyle 0=\ell(\hat{p}_{i},\hat{p}_{j})=\ell_{-\infty}(v,u_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\ell_{\infty}(v,u_{j}^{\prime})\leq a(\hat{x}_{j})=-1,

which is a contradiction. It follows that

\displaystyle\hat{X}

\displaystyle\subseteq\text{supp }h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{p}_{i}).

To conclude that the above containment is in fact an equality, note that in combination with the gluing trick outlined at the beginning of this proof, Proposition
5.18
implies that
\text{supp }h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{p}_{i})\in mp_{(1)}(H)
, but
\hat{X}\in mp_{(1)}(H)
by hypothesis, and so maximality of
\hat{X}
implies

\displaystyle\hat{X}

\displaystyle=\text{supp }h_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{p}_{i})

as desired.
∎

5.3
Dynamical Consequences

Theorem 5.19
.

For any closed symplectic surface
(\Sigma,\omega)
, and any non-degenerate Hamiltonian
H\in C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times\Sigma)
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}_{im}(\tilde{\phi}^{H})

\displaystyle=\min_{\hat{X}\in mp_{(1)}(H)}\max_{\hat{x}\in\hat{X}}\mathcal{A}_{H}(\hat{x})-\max_{\hat{X}\in mn_{(-1)}(H)}\min_{\hat{x}\in\hat{X}}\mathcal{A}_{H}(\hat{x}).

Proof.

Since, for any strongly-semipositive
(M,\omega)
, the PSS-image spectral invariants are well-defined on
\widetilde{Ham}(M,\omega)

\displaystyle\gamma_{im}(\phi)
